					<description><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at Singapore University of Technology and Design (SUTD), A*STAR, and National University of Singapore (NUS) published “Self-Supervised Attention-Based Virtual Metrology for CMP MRR Prediction.” Excerpt &#8220;In this paper, we propose Global Regression Pretraining (GREP), a self-supervised, attention-based framework for CMP* MRR prediction. GREP learns temporal representations from unlabeled sensor signals through global proxy-target regression,... <a class="read_more" href="https://semiengineering.com/self-supervised-virtual-metrology-for-cmp-mrr-prediction-sutd-astar-nus/">&#187; read more</a></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/self-supervised-virtual-metrology-for-cmp-mrr-prediction-sutd-astar-nus/">Self-Supervised Virtual Metrology For CMP MRR Prediction (SUTD, A*STAR, NUS)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at<strong> Singapore University of Technology and Design</strong> (SUTD), <strong>A*STAR</strong>, and <strong>National University of Singapore</strong> (NUS) published “Self-Supervised Attention-Based Virtual Metrology for CMP MRR Prediction.”</p>
<p><strong>Excerpt</strong></p>
<p>&#8220;In this paper, we propose Global Regression Pretraining (GREP), a self-supervised, attention-based framework for CMP* MRR prediction. GREP learns temporal representations from unlabeled sensor signals through global proxy-target regression, followed by supervised fine-tuning for MRR estimation. The framework is evaluated against a feature-engineered supervised baseline under three conditions: limited labeled data, sensor noise, and temporal process drift. &#8220;</p>
<p>*chemical mechanical planarization</p>
<p>Find the <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16172">technical paper here.</a> November 2026.</p>
<p>Rico, Jonathan Adam, Nagarajan Raghavan, and Senthilnath Jayavelu. &#8220;Self-Supervised Attention-Based Virtual Metrology for CMP MRR Prediction.&#8221; arXiv preprint arXiv:2611.16172 (2026). <a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/">License</a> CC BY-NC-ND 4.0.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>

		<title>Reducing High-Bandwidth Flash Latency In GPU Systems (Georgia Tech, Nvidia, Stanford)</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/reducing-high-bandwidth-flash-latency-in-gpu-systems-georgia-tech-nvidia-stanford/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/reducing-high-bandwidth-flash-latency-in-gpu-systems-georgia-tech-nvidia-stanford/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Technical Paper Link]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 16 Nov 2026 23:52:56 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[AI/ML/DL]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Architectures]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Memory]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical Papers]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at Georgia Tech, NVIDIA Research, and Stanford University published “SlipStream: Enabling HBF for GPU Systems by Mitigating the Latency and SRAM Overheads.” Abstract Excerpt &#8220;Large language model (LLM) inference demands ever more memory capacity for larger models and batch sizes. High-Bandwidth Flash (HBF) replaces the DRAM dies of HBM with NAND flash and provides... <a class="read_more" href="https://semiengineering.com/reducing-high-bandwidth-flash-latency-in-gpu-systems-georgia-tech-nvidia-stanford/">&#187; read more</a></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/reducing-high-bandwidth-flash-latency-in-gpu-systems-georgia-tech-nvidia-stanford/">Reducing High-Bandwidth Flash Latency In GPU Systems (Georgia Tech, Nvidia, Stanford)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []">Researchers at <strong>Georgia Tech, NVIDIA Research</strong>, and <strong>Stanford University</strong> published “SlipStream: Enabling HBF for GPU Systems by Mitigating the Latency and SRAM Overheads.”</p>
<p><strong>Abstract Excerpt</strong></p>
<p>&#8220;Large language model (LLM) inference demands ever more memory capacity for larger models and batch sizes. High-Bandwidth Flash (HBF) replaces the DRAM dies of HBM with NAND flash and provides up to 16x the capacity at comparable read bandwidth. However, HBF can incur a read latency of nearly 20<span id="MathJax-Element-1-Frame" class="MathJax" tabindex="0"><span id="MathJax-Span-1" class="math"><span id="MathJax-Span-2" class="mrow"><span id="MathJax-Span-3" class="mi">μ</span></span></span></span>s, 20x that of HBM, so on-demand HBF accesses slow inference by up to 12.8x. Conventional cache-line prefetchers require 20x more in-flight tracking state to cover this latency. Tensor-streaming designs, such as H3, follow virtual-address order as opposed to kernel-demand order, and therefore need hundreds of megabytes of buffering.</p>
<p>We propose SlipStream, a software-hardware co-designed prefetcher that turns prefetching into a demand-paced producer-consumer pipeline. In software, SlipStream profiles the repeatable weight-page demand order of data-independent LLM kernels and inserts a staging warp that replays this order with non-blocking STAGE instructions. In hardware, the staging warp acts as the producer and the work warps as consumers. Our key insight is to retire each STAGE tracking entry only when demand first accesses the page, as opposed to when the page returns from HBF. Demand thus paces staging and bounds how far staging leads consumption. SlipStream tracks 4 KB pages instead of cache lines and allocates buffer slots only when pages return, which decouples the buffer size from outstanding traffic. Across seven dense models spanning 16-800 GB, SlipStream with a 40 MB staging buffer achieves 92.4% of the performance of an ideal HBF with HBM-like latency. H3 achieves only 43.1%, even with a 320 MB buffer. The buffer sizing of SlipStream is independent of HBF latency and workload. SlipStream with 16 MB buffer, 20x smaller than that of H3, performs within 4% of SlipStream-40MB. SlipStream also supports Mixture-of-Experts models and reaches 91.2% of ideal performance (40 MB buffer).&#8221;</p>
<p>Find the technical paper <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14776">here</a>. November 2026.</p>
<p>Saxena, Anish, Hritvik Taneja, Christos Kozyrakis, and Aamer Jaleel. &#8220;SlipStream: Enabling HBF for GPU Systems by Mitigating the Latency and SRAM Overheads.&#8221; arXiv preprint arXiv:2611.14776 (2026). <a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/">License.</a></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/reducing-high-bandwidth-flash-latency-in-gpu-systems-georgia-tech-nvidia-stanford/">Reducing High-Bandwidth Flash Latency In GPU Systems (Georgia Tech, Nvidia, Stanford)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>What Is Acoustic Micro Imaging? A Quick Guide To This Inspection Process</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/what-is-acoustic-micro-imaging-a-quick-guide-to-this-inspection-process/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/what-is-acoustic-micro-imaging-a-quick-guide-to-this-inspection-process/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rebecca McCordic]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 16 Nov 2026 08:01:29 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - PTES]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Inspecting The Future]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Test, Measurement and Analytics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[acoustic micro imaging]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[inspection]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Nordson]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ultrasound]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[X-ray inspection]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284228</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Obtaining clear images of gaps and delaminations in advanced packages.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/what-is-acoustic-micro-imaging-a-quick-guide-to-this-inspection-process/">What Is Acoustic Micro Imaging? A Quick Guide To This Inspection Process</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>We all know how important inspection is for advanced packaging. Not only are these processes critical for ensuring higher yield, but they’re also integral for maintaining device reliability. As the industry pushes for smaller features and tighter spacing, even the smallest defects can pose major challenges. For instance, delaminations and air gaps can be detrimental in a market that demands increasingly complex devices. Such defects will lead to electrical or mechanical failures down the road.</p>
<p>One technology that’s mitigating these issues is acoustic micro imaging (AMI). AMI is a high-frequency ultrasound technology, and it’s being used by the industry as an inspection method.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" fetchpriority="high" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284229" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig1.webp?resize=1000%2C500&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1000" height="500" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig1.webp?w=1000&amp;ssl=1 1000w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig1.webp?resize=300%2C150&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig1.webp?resize=768%2C384&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig1.webp?resize=600%2C300&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<h2>How AMI works</h2>
<p>At the core of AMI technology is a transducer, or a device that turns energy from one form to another. It starts with a pulse of electrical energy, then converts it to mechanical energy, or a high-frequency ultrasound wave. Because ultrasound doesn’t travel through air, this allows inspectors to obtain clear images of gaps and delaminations in the device that could otherwise be difficult to see.</p>
<p>However, the transducers used for AMI are not all the same. Each transducer handles a specific frequency, and they’re chosen based on the sample that needs to be inspected. By moving the transducer across the sample, the inspector can generate multiple images that can then be analyzed by the AMI software. Through this analysis, the inspector can choose to accept or reject parts based on the number of defects the package is found to have.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284230" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig2.webp?resize=700%2C423&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="700" height="423" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig2.webp?w=700&amp;ssl=1 700w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig2.webp?resize=300%2C181&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig2.webp?resize=600%2C363&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 700px) 100vw, 700px" /></p>
<p>So, how do these defects typically present themselves?</p>
<p>The biggest difference between ideal and defective samples is that some defective samples will have very clear air gaps. However, another common defect is thinning of the attach material on solder pads. When this material is too thin, any stress that would be put on the device could lead to delamination. An uneven solder or thermal interface material (TIM) layer may also indicate a larger problem with the build process.</p>
<h2>When to use AMI – inspection, destructive testing, and X-rays</h2>
<p>With the ability to gather so much information without damaging the device, why would anyone choose to perform a destructive test to identify a defect, rather than simply use AMI inspection to check for them? One reason is for R&amp;D purposes. Oftentimes, companies want to ensure that a process is fully developed before integrating it into their production cycle, and a destructive test can confirm proof of concept for AMI.</p>
<p>An example of this in action is a standard capacitor inspection. New capacitor companies will often require a destructive test to confirm the presence of voids or delaminations found by the AMI software. Once the destructive proof of concept has been completed, the company can then integrate the software into their inspection process.</p>
<p>There are some key differences between AMI and its transmission technology counterpart, which is more comparable to X-ray. Whereas AMI is an ultrasound technology, transmission scanning uses both a sending and receiving transducer positioned above and below the sample. Both transmission technology and X-ray create composite images, unlike AMI, which inspects layer-by-layer.</p>
<p>Ultrasound and X-ray are complementary technologies. There are some things AMI can do that X-ray can’t, and vice versa. For certain applications, both technologies may be recommended. For example, composite images are preferable for inspecting spherical objects like ball grid arrays.</p>
<h2>The challenges and future of AMI</h2>
<p>Although AMI technology has tangible benefits, keeping up with the industry’s rapid pace is a hurdle that AMI must continually overcome. As applications become more complex, AMI software needs to quickly adapt to changing components. For instance, instead of scanning a single flip chip, AMI software could now be required to inspect multi-die stacks.</p>
<p>One way this mounting complexity is being addressed is through machine learning algorithms.</p>
<p>For example, some wafers have via patterns that look similar or identical to delamination, and humans have typically been responsible for distinguishing them. However, through advancements in machine learning, AMI software can now identify via patterns and exclude those from the defect count. As machine learning becomes more robust, it will open new doors for inspecting increasingly complicated devices.</p>
<p>In addition, customer requirements for higher resolution and better throughput are also driving new developments for AMI software. To meet these rising demands, Nordson Test &amp; Inspection developed its automated SpinSAM tool that can inspect four 300mm wafers at a time.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284231" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig3.webp?resize=700%2C620&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="700" height="620" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig3.webp?w=700&amp;ssl=1 700w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig3.webp?resize=300%2C266&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Nordson_What-is-Acoustic-Micro-Imaging-fig3.webp?resize=600%2C531&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 700px) 100vw, 700px" /></p>
<p>Traditionally, acoustic microscopy systems move the transducer in a raster pattern, which is a small grid made up of individual pixels. This approach allows inspectors to collect data at every XY point, but it also requires decelerating to turn the transducer at the end of each line, thereby reducing throughput. Some acoustic microscopes address the need for better throughput by skipping lines and interpolating data points, or by using multiple transducers and stitching images together – both of which have their own drawbacks.</p>
<p>In contrast, <a href="https://www.nordson.com/en/products/test-and-inspection-products/ami-spinsam">SpinSAM</a> spins the wafer to move the transducer and the wafer continuously, allowing inspectors to scan each pixel faster, allowing for better throughput without image stitching distortion.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/what-is-acoustic-micro-imaging-a-quick-guide-to-this-inspection-process/">What Is Acoustic Micro Imaging? A Quick Guide To This Inspection Process</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Are AI Agents The New Semiconductor IP?</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/are-ai-agents-the-new-semiconductor-ip/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/are-ai-agents-the-new-semiconductor-ip/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ed Sperling]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 16 Nov 2026 08:01:17 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Low Power-High Performance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Special Reports]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[agentic AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ARM]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Cadence]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ChipAgents]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Cognichip]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Keysight EDA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Movellus]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[semiconductor IP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Siemens EDA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Silvaco]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284262</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Agents are emerging as reusable design tools, but trust and use models remain challenging.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/are-ai-agents-the-new-semiconductor-ip/">Are AI Agents The New Semiconductor IP?</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>AI agents could make customized design workflows faster and less expensive to develop.</li>
<li>Multi-agent systems introduce new challenges for orchestration, verification, and tool integration.</li>
<li>Consumption-based pricing could reshape EDA business models, but trust and proprietary data remain obstacles.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>AI agents are emerging as a new category of reusable design capability, but with a twist. Rather than providing tightly defined design blocks, agents can be used to explore different options, integrate them, and use those changes to improve designs.</p>
<p>At the leading edge, soft IP developers have been customizing their code for large customers for more than a decade to boost performance and/or reduce power. But that customization is typically narrow and costly, and optimizations for one design may not directly transfer to derivative chips — especially multi-die assemblies at the leading edge.</p>
<p>Agents, in contrast, can be used to customize different functions with little or no human intervention, and they can self-optimize for different workloads and use cases, and some agents can even adjust for aging hardware. And they can do all of this much faster than a human, even though an experienced engineering team is needed to verify those results and recommendations.</p>
<p>&#8220;We have in-house specialized AI agents to do different things, to do spec analysis, RTL generation for design, for formal and functional verification, and then CDC linking, and place-and-route,&#8221; said William Wang, CEO of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/alpha-design-ai-chipagents/">ChipAgents</a>. &#8220;We provide our optimized agents to a customer, and sometimes a customer builds their own AI agent. But there are some similarities with the IP business. Sometimes we&#8217;re specifically building an agent for one customer for a particular flow, which is similar to what happens in the IP business. What&#8217;s different is that we can do it in a very short amount of time. With IP, you need 10 months. Our cycle is much shorter because AI agents are very easy to customize. In some cases we can give solutions to the customer the same day.&#8221;</p>
<p>Those solutions still need to be verified and debugged. But the ability to sort through many different options quickly is an attractive formula, especially with rapid evolution of AI.</p>
<p>&#8220;I draw parallels to the early 2000s gigahertz wars, where all of a sudden everything was multicore,&#8221; said Mo Faisal, CEO of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/movellus/">Movellus</a>. &#8220;Now we&#8217;ve gone from,&#8217; Everything is going to be a GPU,&#8217; to &#8216;Oh, no, it&#8217;s CPUs and GPUs, and this is a one-to-one ratio.&#8217; So effectively we&#8217;re going through the same architectural churn that we go through every 10 to 15 years.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>The business of agents</strong><br />
Business models still need to solidify around agents. but they also need to be flexible enough to evolve at the same pace as the technology.</p>
<p>&#8220;If you look at how the EDA industry has evolved, we&#8217;re not selling perpetual licenses anymore,&#8221; said Rob Knoth, senior group director of Strategy &amp; New Ventures at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/cadence-design-systems/">Cadence</a>. &#8220;We moved to time-based licenses many years ago. Now we see similar things as agents roll around. They&#8217;re hitting up the tool usage even more because they want to find answers quickly, and they&#8217;re trying lots of experiments. So putting together more of a consumption-based model is really good because customers know how to use that.&#8221;</p>
<p>This isn&#8217;t a simple transition, though. While the promise of agents is significant, their adoption in chip design will take time. In addition, security and permissions aren&#8217;t always well-defined.</p>
<p>&#8220;There are some things where you&#8217;d really rather sell an agent than sell the software,&#8221; said Wally Rhines, CEO of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/silvaco-inc/">Silvaco</a>. &#8220;People are trying to figure out if anybody in EDA is actually pricing based upon tokens rather than traditional pricing models. It doesn&#8217;t seem like anyone is, but their customers are all talking about it. I&#8217;m very much open to selling an agent that will automate [manufacturing equipment] calibration, then jump back to my store of synthetic simulated data against the physical data. We don&#8217;t get to see the physical data, but we do know how many tokens our agents consumed. It&#8217;s a way that EDA can sell a product, get the benefit, and no one ever sees the underlying data that companies want to keep proprietary.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Divide and conquer, but with caveats</strong><br />
One of the big changes with the shift from generative AI to agentic AI is the need for orchestration, and this has broad business implications. Orchestration is basically a blueprint that defines how tasks are assigned, how agents access tools and data, and how that work is coordinated. But it&#8217;s especially challenging if multiple agents execute at the same time and their actions overlap.</p>
<p>&#8220;What&#8217;s different about traditional AI and agentic AI is the coordination of multiple agents that are working in parallel, but doing different types of work,&#8221; said Robbie Williamson, vice president of customer engineering for cloud AI at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/arm/">Arm</a>. &#8220;It&#8217;s not like there&#8217;s a unique workload that we haven&#8217;t seen before. It&#8217;s more about how do you coordinate all of these at the CPU level, and have enough bandwidth, memory, etc., to do all of this at scale. Instead of an individual making a request and driving AI, we have computers that can do it much faster. The transition that we see when we talk to hyperscalers is now about what they need from the CPU. With training, that wasn&#8217;t as important because you&#8217;re just batching the job and waiting for it to come back.&#8221;</p>
<p>That creates a new hierarchy in the design flow. &#8220;What has happened with this multi-agent workflow is that, in an organization, you&#8217;re trying to design a chip,&#8221; said Prith Banerjee, senior vice president of innovation at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>. &#8220;When they&#8217;re having a problem with the PPA, some engineers say they know exactly what changes to make to lower the power. They&#8217;ll do clock gating. But when you clock gate power, then it changes something else. It increases the area. So some area will come in — and this is exactly what happens in the real world with coordination when multiple people come together to design a system. The human manager is coordinating the activities of the human agents in the world of a multi-agent framework. We have agents for EDA tools and agents for the CAE (computer-aided engineering) tools. We have simulation with three core solvers — CFD (computational fluid dynamics), HFSS (high-frequency structure simulation) for the electromagnetics, and mechanical for the structural part. In the EDA world, we have things for the implementation, and things for sign-off. Each of these tools has sub-areas, and we have agents to do these things. But what was happening by a team of engineers coordinated by a human manager is now being done by a hierarchy of agents with that framework.&#8221;</p>
<p>That changes some of the dynamics in chip design in not-so-subtle ways. Soft IP is often portable across process technologies, but it typically requires optimization and validation. AI agents can automate those tasks by coordinating EDA tools and weighing alternatives, and they can work with traditional IP to optimize specific functions in the context of a larger design.</p>
<p>&#8220;One of the key implications of agentic workflows is disaggregation,&#8221; said Niels Faché, senior vice president for <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/keysight-technologies/">Keysight</a>&#8216;s Design Engineering Software business. &#8220;We believe in platforms. Now we have to see how everybody&#8217;s going to deal with that because a platform is a very sticky concept. Many of our customers invest in customizing our platform for their needs. It&#8217;s very hard to move away from it. It&#8217;s the same for Cadence and Synopsys, but we believe that ultimately customers want that flexibility. We take an open approach to this, where we accept that disaggregation is going to happen. We need to play to the strength of our simulation capability and our understanding of the application. Customers still wanting to work with us on creating their workflows will need help with orchestration, so we need to be experts in that domain, while the most sophisticated customers will do it themselves.&#8221;</p>
<p>That requires some fundamental changes for EDA companies. &#8220;With AI, that&#8217;s a different model where human engineers work with digital engineers or agents,&#8221; Faché said. &#8220;So now we have to redesign our products. You&#8217;ve heard about MCP (Model Context Protocol) servers where we look at a product and ask, &#8216;Okay, how can we modularize it to provide access to different capabilities with the proper APIs?&#8217; All of that coding is typically done in Python. That would then allow an agent to stitch together a code-based workflow. Our job is to make sure that our customers get this MCP functionality. We also provide examples so that an agent can learn from them and say, &#8216;Okay, based on these examples, I understand how this works. And now I&#8217;ve got a request for a new design, and I can organize that work based on what I&#8217;ve learned.&#8217; That&#8217;s the world we&#8217;re moving toward, and we already see that with our front-runner customers. They are already building these agentic workflows. That means we need MCP servers, and we need all the proper documentation so we can train these agents.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>What else can agents do?</strong><br />
How much freedom to give agents isn&#8217;t entirely clear. AI is opaque, and unlike traditional IP, it needs to be observed from the outside. This becomes more complicated as more agents are assigned to different but connected jobs.</p>
<p>&#8220;Orchestration is going to be a big challenge, and it will become an even bigger challenge,&#8221; said Stelios Diamantidis<strong>, </strong>chief product officer at Cognichip. &#8220;As agents become smarter, they can deliver some outcomes, and it&#8217;s been proven to work in many other spaces, as well. But putting all these agents together to drive them as a cohort toward an answer to a problem can also have some interesting challenges. We&#8217;re still working with models that generally do not understand the deep physics of the chip design problem, and that means a lot of the logic being baked into agents. But what happens when those agents begin to disagree? Are we going to have ping-pong effects and convergence issues in EDA? We&#8217;re no strangers to those effects.&#8221;</p>
<p>This is particularly true with multi-die assemblies. &#8220;The stack is very heterogeneous,&#8221; Diamantidis said. &#8220;You&#8217;re looking at a model that was trained with sources that generally we don&#8217;t know very much about. You have agents that have been given enough logic about a problem by enough different people that the combination of agents and models is now a fairly chaotic model. You put them all together and you get chaos times chaos.&#8221;</p>
<p>These challenges help explain why IP and EDA companies are working to tightly integrate agents into existing tools.</p>
<p>&#8220;When you look at how we&#8217;re applying agents into our data center design and operation tooling, agentic AI coupled with physics-based simulation is great at being able to integrate large amounts of disparate data, ask questions, run experiments, evaluate the output of those experiments, and then do the loop again,&#8221; said Cadence&#8217;s Knoth. &#8220;And what&#8217;s very unique about the space we operate in is that, as opposed to other industries, we don&#8217;t need a person to look at the results and say that&#8217;s right or that&#8217;s wrong. EDA has the tools that we&#8217;ve used for decades to grade the decisions. &#8216;That floor plan worked better. It lowered the power.&#8217; Or, &#8216;That floor plan was worse. The power went up, the heating was worse.'&#8221;</p>
<p>Establishing a hierarchy for agents is important, but it&#8217;s not always straightforward. &#8220;So you have one agent with different sub-agents to do different things, and then we have a multi-agent solution,&#8221; said Wang. &#8220;We released a multi-agent solution last year. So for debugging, your SoC is so big that it makes it hard to optimize performance. You need multiple agents to have parallel access to different contexts to do the job. Similarly, with formal, you have multiple agents looking at doing the debugging and being able to optimize the design. Those are extremely critical. So you need multi-agent coordination. In the future, we&#8217;re going to have virtual chip companies with virtual AI engineers working together, collaborating from this agent organization, and then maybe a part of the chip companies will be virtual in the future.&#8221;</p>
<p>That, in turn, could accelerate the adoption of agents.</p>
<p>&#8220;There could be a time where we start looking at agentic workloads and try to figure out how we can model those in pre-silicon to improve IP around that, and what that means in terms of the design of the CPU itself and our CSS offerings for those building their own,&#8221; said Arm&#8217;s Williamson.</p>
<p><strong>Trust and verification</strong><br />
The bigger question is how much autonomy chip designers will be willing to give agents.</p>
<p>&#8220;The cost of a mistake is $500 million at 3 or 2 nanometers,&#8221; said Ankur Gupta, executive vice president of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/mentor-a-siemens-business/">Siemens EDA</a> IC products. &#8220;That&#8217;s why these engines have to remain deterministic. They have to remain high-fidelity. We, of course, use reinforcement learning, unsupervised learning, and we&#8217;ve been doing that for more than a decade. But the way we measure the result of that learning capability is whether we are still delivering a trusted outcome. So that doesn&#8217;t change.&#8221;</p>
<p>There&#8217;s more to the trust equation than just the outcome, though. It harkens back at least a few decades to the challenge of sharing process data that chipmakers and chip companies consider to be their IP.</p>
<p>&#8220;All of these agent technologies and models are pretty good at understanding the language components,&#8221; said Gupta. &#8220;But the physics component is still the big challenge. And if you think about SLMs, LLMs, where are they going to get the vast amounts of data from, because the whole universe of semiconductor and electronics designs is very siloed and customers are concerned about their IP. Everybody wants on-prem, but that argument persists because you&#8217;ve got the foundry, which is not giving up anything.&#8221;</p>
<p>Foundries do share PDKs with end customers and EDA companies, but they tightly restrict access to them, which makes it more difficult to train AI and to share models. And because AI is opaque, it becomes even more difficult to convince people to share data.</p>
<p>Nevertheless, the adoption of agents continues to grow. &#8220;The analogy is to what the human engineer is doing in a company,&#8221; said Synopsys&#8217; Banerjee. &#8220;You do not have a single engineer doing the entire design of a chip or a system. You have teams of engineers, and teams of engineers are managed by managers, by directors, by vice presidents. And you have that hierarchy because individuals are doing different things, which they are skilled at. Then the manager coordinates their work. So the early work in AI applied to EDA was to make the tools go faster, better PPA. This is what we did with our DSO.ai (design space optimization). The early work on AI was GenAI. It was generating a script. So you ask a question. &#8216;I am struggling with this Fusion Compiler. What setting should I use?&#8217; And based on hundreds of questions and answers, this thing essentially is a ChatGPT targeted to this particular line of questions. So it&#8217;s an agent for RTL assistance, or whatever.&#8221;</p>
<p>AI agents can even be used to fine-tune existing steps in the design flow. &#8220;In general, we want to have some very commonly used workflow agents,&#8221; said ChipAgents&#8217; Wang. &#8220;For example, when you&#8217;re building an accelerator chip, it&#8217;s very different from a CPU. So how do you do verification? Do you do more formal, less formal? It&#8217;s a very different methodology. Having that flexibility is really critical. You don&#8217;t want to build a workflow that is very rigid so people cannot customize it. The platform needs to allow you to customize for your specific chip while also being general enough.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Conclusion</strong><br />
&#8220;The model brings a lot of intelligence, so the agent doesn&#8217;t have to be terribly smart,&#8221; said Cognichip&#8217;s Diamantidis. &#8220;It can be there to introduce some guardrails and add some additional skills. But right now we&#8217;re flipping it. We&#8217;re saying, &#8216;You know, we don&#8217;t have billions of examples of hardware, and they&#8217;re spread out in all these different silos. So we can&#8217;t get them in one place. We can train a model that&#8217;s very focused on what it does and put everything on the agent, but that makes it top-heavy, and top-heavy things tend to lose their balance. That&#8217;s one of the big challenges.&#8221;</p>
<p>So even though AI agents are being integrated into traditional workflows, chip design is still cautious about when and where to deploy it.</p>
<p>&#8220;Should agents be managing the hardware that is running all kinds of agents? The answer is yes,&#8221; said Movellus&#8217; Faisal. &#8220;Is that going to be easy? I don&#8217;t think so, because the silicon industry that is enabling the intelligence and adopting all of that for internal tooling and even management is probably the slowest and most conservative. A $500 million silicon program failure is a big deal. There are going to be all kinds of management agents that will be needed — managing the rack, managing thermals, managing workload orchestration.&#8221;</p>
<p>While agentic AI won&#8217;t replace traditional IP, it could impact the design process in which that IP is used. Change is coming, but there are still a lot of challenges and unknowns ahead.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/are-ai-agents-the-new-semiconductor-ip/">Are AI Agents The New Semiconductor IP?</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Tharunya Yogananda and Wantong Li of the University of California, Riverside published a technical paper titled “Beyond Host-Side Control: HBM Controller Partitioning Across Host and HBM Base Dies.” Abstract Excerpt: &#8220;Recent generations of high bandwidth memory (HBM), such as HBM4, allow the base die of the memory stack to be manufactured using a logic process... <a class="read_more" href="https://semiengineering.com/shifting-hbm4-controller-functions-closer-to-memory-uc-riverside/">&#187; read more</a></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/shifting-hbm4-controller-functions-closer-to-memory-uc-riverside/">Shifting HBM4 Controller Functions Closer To Memory (UC Riverside)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Tharunya Yogananda and Wantong Li of the <strong>University of California, Riverside</strong> published a technical paper titled “Beyond Host-Side Control: HBM Controller Partitioning Across Host and HBM Base Dies.”</p>
<p><strong>Abstract Excerpt</strong>:</p>
<p>&#8220;Recent generations of high bandwidth memory (HBM), such as HBM4, allow the base die of the memory stack to be manufactured using a logic process that can accommodate more complex controller functions next to the DRAM. We present the first systematic quantitative study of function-level controller placement between the host and the HBM4 base die on a silicon interposer. We divide the controller into its main functions and evaluate four placements, from a complete host controller to a complete base die controller, with intermediate placements that move maintenance or scheduling to the base die. &#8220;</p>
<p>Find the <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10930">technical paper here</a>. November 2026.</p>
<p>Yogananda, Tharunya, and Wantong Li. “Beyond Host-Side Control: HBM Controller Partitioning Across Host and HBM Base Dies.” arXiv:2611.10930, November 2026. <a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/">License.</a></p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>300mm SiC breakthrough; Nvidia faces questions about security and oversight; Siemens buys Lauterbach; chiplet standards; new memory bets; imec interposer process flow; TPUs with CPUs; 2D transistors; anti-smuggling tools for chips.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/chip-industry-week-in-review-164/">Chip Industry Week In Review</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<h3><strong>Innovations</strong></h3>
<p>A wave of announcements this week, including several from <strong>SEMICON Europa</strong> and <strong>electronica 2026</strong> in Munich, highlighted advances in semiconductor manufacturing, advanced packaging, automotive chips, and AI hardware.</p>
<h4 style="margin: 16px 0 0 0; padding: 0;">     <span style="text-decoration: underline;">Manufacturing</span></h4>
<ul style="margin-top: 0; padding-top: 0;">
<li><strong>Infineon</strong> reported an <a href="https://www.infineon.com/press-release/2026/infpr202611-028">industry-first milestone in 300mm silicon carbide manufacturing</a>, fabricating functional power devices at its Villach, Austria fab using existing silicon processing equipment. The larger wafers can accommodate 2.3 times as many chips as 200mm wafers, potentially lowering manufacturing costs for automotive, industrial, and AI data center power devices.</li>
<li><strong>imec</strong> completed baseline process flows for its <a href="https://www.imec-int.com/en/press/imec-advances-europes-silicon-interposer-technology-capabilities-through-nanoic-pilot-line">NanoIC silicon-interposer platform</a>, including TSVs and multiple routing layers for chiplet and 3D integration. A silicon-interposer PDK is planned for early 2027.</li>
</ul>
<h4 style="margin: 16px 0 0 0; padding: 0;">     <span style="text-decoration: underline;">Chips and systems</span></h4>
<ul style="margin-top: 0; padding-top: 0;">
<li><strong>AMD</strong> introduced its 6th-gen EPYC <a href="https://newsroom.amd.com/news/6th-gen-epyc-lead-ai-enterprise-computing/">server</a> and <a href="https://newsroom.amd.com/news/2026-in-depth-embedded-epyc-venice/">embedded</a> processors, including models for semiconductor ATE; an <a href="https://newsroom.amd.com/news/amd-advances-instinct-mi400-series-ai-scientific-computing/">AI GPU</a> with 288GB of HBM4; and <a href="https://newsroom.amd.com/news/amd-advances-built-in-protection-physical-attacks-security-critical-systems/">tamper-resistant FPGAs</a> for security-critical systems.</li>
<li><strong>Arm</strong> introduced the Cortex-M42 processor to bring <a href="https://newsroom.arm.com/news/arm-cortex-m42-accelerate-edge-ai-innovation">ML and DSP</a> capabilities to cost- and area-constrained intelligent endpoints in a compact, AHB-based design.</li>
<li><strong>Google</strong> added two new 8th-gen <a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/infrastructure-and-cloud/global-network/tpu-8i-max-and-8tx/">TPU systems</a>: TPU 8i Max for massive context windows and higher rack compute density, and TPU 8tx for secure and confidential inference. TPU 8tx pairs Google accelerators with AMD EPYC CPU hosts.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Fallout continues after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang&#8217;s <a href="https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-announces-ceo-transition">resignation</a>:</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Congressional investigations:</strong> Senators are seeking the <a href="https://www.banking.senate.gov/imo/media/doc/20261111_warren_coons_letter_to_wh_commerce_state_re_nvidia_record_retentionpdf.pdf">preservation of records</a> related to chip exports and Megaspeed, calling for tighter AI-chip <a href="https://www.banking.senate.gov/newsroom/minority/warren-banks-statement-on-nvidias-continued-export-of-advanced-ai-chips-following-reports-of-foreign-intelligence-operation">export controls</a>. House committee leaders also requested a <a href="https://foreignaffairs.house.gov/sites/evo-subsites/republicans-foreignaffairs.house.gov/files/evo-media-document/11.4.26nvidialetter.pdf">closed-door Nov. 18 briefing</a> from Huang.</li>
<li><strong>Inaction questioned:</strong> President Trump authorized tougher action on suspected Nvidia chip diversion three times this year, but Commerce Secretary <strong>Howard Lutnick</strong> resisted over concerns that tighter restrictions could escalate <a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/11/11/trump-lutnick-nvidia-china-chip-export-controls">trade tensions with China</a>, Axios reported. Separately, Nvidia general counsel <strong>Tim Teter</strong> <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-11-09/nvidia-intervened-in-us-probe-of-megaspeed-chip-smuggling">contacted a senior Commerce Dept. official</a> in late summer 2025 seeking information about the Megaspeed chip-smuggling investigation, indicating Nvidia&#8217;s legal leadership was aware of the investigation before the current scandal. The company subsequently characterized its Megaspeed dealings as “<a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-10-29/nvidia-ceo-says-ties-to-company-under-spying-probe-were-normal">normal</a>.”</li>
<li><strong>Circular financing:</strong> Nvidia’s <a href="https://www.ft.com/content/dd9072bd-e8a3-4f8e-9985-550aa80a2fd4">web of financial ties across</a> the AI industry is facing fresh scrutiny after Huang’s resignation. The Financial Times says Nvidia made more than 120 investments this year and held or committed about $124B in equity investments by July, raising questions about whether its aggressive dealmaking and financing of companies that buy its chips will continue without him.</li>
<li><strong>Policy fallout:</strong> The episode has renewed debate over export-control enforcement and Chinese intelligence targeting of U.S. technology companies, with <strong>CFR</strong> highlighting <a href="https://www.cfr.org/articles/nvidia-chinese-espionage-and-the-ai-race-four-foreign-policy-issues-to-watch">enforcement gaps</a> and <strong>CSET</strong> urging <a href="https://cset.georgetown.edu/article/espionage-ai-and-the-risks-of-strategic-overreaction/">scrutiny without overreaction</a>.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Deals and funding</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Siemens</strong> plans to <a href="https://press.siemens.com/global/en/pressrelease/siemens-expand-portfolio-semiconductor-design-and-verification-planned-acquisition">acquire</a> <strong>Lauterbach</strong>, a provider of hardware-assisted debugging and real-time trace tools for embedded software and complex SoCs. The deal is expected to close in H1 2027.</li>
<li><strong>Mitsui Chemicals</strong> confirmed it will <a href="https://ap.mitsuichemicals.com/content/dam/mitsuichemicals/sites/mci/documents/release/2026/261109e.pdf">acquire JSR</a> in a transaction valued at approximately ¥1.84T ($11.9B), combining two major Japanese materials suppliers, including JSR’s photoresist and advanced materials businesses.</li>
<li><strong>TSMC</strong> approved <a href="https://pr.tsmc.com/english/news/3352">investments of up to $1B in OpenAI and Anthropic</a>, allocating up to $500M for each, plus a separate $500M investment company focused on the semiconductor ecosystem.</li>
<li><strong>Fractile</strong> raised $720M to develop frontier AI chips that <a href="https://www.balderton.com/news/fractile-raises-720m-to-build-the-hardware-that-pushes-ais-frontier-forward/">stack DRAM</a> directly on top of logic to speed up output token generation.</li>
</ul>
<p><a href="https://vitrek.com/understanding-semiconductor-wafer-stress-a-practical-guide-to-thin-film-stress-wafer-curvature-measurement-process-control/?utm_source=email&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_campaign=M-Semiconductor+Engineering-News+Alert+Enewsletter-Wafer+Stress+WP-2026-09"><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone wp-image-24281991 size-full" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Oct26-news-alert-Understanding-Wafer-Stress-1270x240-1.jpg?resize=1270%2C240&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1270" height="240" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Oct26-news-alert-Understanding-Wafer-Stress-1270x240-1.jpg?w=1270&amp;ssl=1 1270w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Oct26-news-alert-Understanding-Wafer-Stress-1270x240-1.jpg?resize=300%2C57&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Oct26-news-alert-Understanding-Wafer-Stress-1270x240-1.jpg?resize=1024%2C194&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Oct26-news-alert-Understanding-Wafer-Stress-1270x240-1.jpg?resize=768%2C145&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Oct26-news-alert-Understanding-Wafer-Stress-1270x240-1.jpg?resize=600%2C113&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></a></p>
<h3><strong>Memory</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Samsung Electronics</strong> broke ground on a previously announced <a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/asia-pacific/south-koreas-lee-says-samsung-chip-packaging-expansion-spur-30-billion-2026-11-12/">HBM advanced-packaging facility</a> in <strong>Asan, South Korea</strong>, accelerating the planned start of production by a year to June 2028.</li>
<li><strong>TSMC</strong> is reportedly <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-11-06/tsmc-mulls-making-advanced-ai-memory-chips-information-says">considering 3D DRAM production</a> at <strong>Nvidia</strong>’s request, potentially through new capacity or an acquired fab. Still, TSMC said that it remains focused on its core logic foundry business.</li>
<li><strong>Vertical Compute</strong> and <strong>imec</strong> expanded their collaboration to integrate <a href="https://verticalcompute.com/media/vertical-compute-expands-partnership-with-imec-to-accelerate-commercial-manufacturing-of-vertically-integrated-memory-vim/">magnetic memory directly above 16nm logic</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Winbond</strong> approved NT$73.1B (US$2.2B) in spending to <a href="https://www.winbond.com/export/sites/winbond/about-winbond/investor/investor-conference/3Q26-investor-conference_CH_Final.pdf">expand its 300mm memory fab</a> in <strong>Kaohsiung, Taiwan</strong>.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Reports and analysis</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Lowy Institute</strong> estimates U.S. <a href="https://www.lowyinstitute.org/publications/asia-power-snapshot-china-the-united-states-and-the-race-for-ai-dominance">AI data centers have nearly 40 times China’s computing power</a>, with U.S. AI chips providing about 31 times the compute. China, meanwhile, leads in critical-mineral production and downloads of open AI models.</li>
<li><strong>CSIS</strong> says Chinese <a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/one-year-after-busan-has-china-kept-its-rare-earth-promises">rare-earth shipments to the U.S. remain about 75% below pre-restriction levels</a>, despite agreements intended to restore supply. U.S. chipmakers have also faced scandium shortages and delays in obtaining Chinese export licenses.</li>
<li><strong>Counterpoint Research</strong> says soaring <a href="https://counterpointresearch.com/en/reports/memory-price-crisis-great-threat-to-affordability">memory prices are driving up smartphone costs</a>, with memory now accounting for 54% of a low-end phone’s bill of materials, up from 20% in Q3 2025. Prices are expected to keep rising through Q1 2027.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Chiplet standards and interoperability</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li>The <strong>UCIe Consortium</strong> published <a href="https://www.uciexpress.org/_files/ugd/0c1418_970201e6ba294a0fa690ae864082fc46.pdf">UCIe 4.0,</a> which takes the specification beyond chiplet-level interfaces to the integration of multiple chiplets into an on-package system. New features include an on-package memory link between SoCs and memory logic chiplets, co-optimized link profiles with improved energy efficiency, and enhanced manageability, security, and reliability.</li>
<li><strong>ASRA</strong> and <strong>imec</strong> released <a href="https://www.imec-int.com/en/press/asra-and-imec-release-joint-architectural-requirements-interoperable-automotive-chiplets">requirements for interoperable automotive chiplets</a>, covering connectivity, power management, and functional safety.</li>
</ul>

<li><strong>Nexperia</strong> is seeking <a href="https://www.nexperia.com/about/news-events/press-releases/Statement-from-Nexperia-november-2026">$2B in damages</a> from Chinese parent <strong>Wingtech</strong> and related parties, alleging harm to its business.</li>
<li><strong>CEA-Leti</strong> and <strong>ISRL</strong> launched <strong>SubFAB Labs</strong> in <strong>Grenoble, </strong>a platform for testing technologies that reduce the <a href="https://www.leti-cea.com/cea-tech/leti/english/Pages/What%27s-On/Press%20release/PR_SubFAB.aspx">environmental impact of chip manufacturing</a>.</li>

<li><strong>Mitsui Kinzoku</strong> plans to invest ¥190B (~US$1.2B) in a <a href="https://www.mitsui-kinzoku.com/LinkClick.aspx?fileticket=eZoFgO6NGE8=&amp;tabid=204&amp;mid=1027&amp;">new copper foil plant</a> in <strong>Malaysia</strong> for high-frequency circuit boards.</li>

<h2 id="New Technologies"><strong>New Technologies</strong></h2>
<h3><strong>Design, verification and test</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Accellera</strong> published the <a href="https://www.accellera.org/news/press-releases/434-accellera-approves-portable-stimulus-standard-3-1-for-release">Portable Test and Stimulus Standard</a> (PSS) 3.1, adding enhancements that improve model reuse, constraint expressiveness, software integration, target-code generation, and coordination across increasingly complex hardware and software execution environments.</li>
<li><strong>Teradyne</strong> introduced its Omnyx Edge inline <a href="https://investors.teradyne.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/456/teradyne-introduces-omnyx-edge-inline-board-test-for-physical-and-edge-ai">board-test system for physical and edge AI</a> applications, designed to improve test coverage and manufacturing efficiency for increasingly complex electronic assemblies.</li>
<li><strong>Siemens</strong> announced a toolkit for <a href="https://news.siemens.com/en-us/siemens-hyperlynx-agentic-toolkit/">signal and power integrity</a> verification of boards and electronic systems that combines domain-specific AI agents for SerDes, DDR, signal integrity, and power integrity analysis with physics-based simulation.</li>
<li><strong>Cadence</strong> launched a connected <a href="https://www.cadence.com/en_US/home/company/newsroom/press-releases/pr/2026/cadence-orcad-xplore-design-platform-unifies-design-component.html">design environment</a> for boards and electronic systems that unifies schematic design, component discovery, expertise, and procurement for individual engineers and small design teams.</li>
<li><strong>Keysight</strong>&#8216;s photonic design software added support for <strong>Tower Semiconductor</strong>&#8216;s PH18 <a href="https://www.keysight.com/us/en/about/newsroom/catalog/news-release.2026.1109_pr26-120-keysight-brings-radio-frequency-and-photonic-design-together-for-tower-semiconductor.html">silicon photonics</a> PDKs.</li>
<li><strong>Synopsys</strong> supplied <strong>Infineon</strong> with a <a href="https://news.synopsys.com/2026-11-10-Synopsys-Advances-Virtual-Development-of-Next-Generation-Automotive-and-Physical-AI-Applications-with-Infineon-AURIX-TM-RC1-Microcontroller-Family">virtual development tool</a> to support digital twin engineering for Infineon’s new MCUs for advanced automotive and physical AI applications.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Lithography and materials</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Canon</strong> introduced a new <a href="https://global.canon/en/news/2026/20261110.html">KrF lithography scanner</a> for memory and logic chip manufacturing. Scheduled for launch in early December, the system offers throughput of up to 400 300mm wafers per hour and 4.0 nm overlay accuracy. It also accommodates wafer warpage of up to 700 microns, addressing manufacturing challenges associated with 3D device structures and wafer bonding.</li>
<li><strong>Hummink</strong> introduced its BIRD module for <a href="https://hummink.com/hummink-unveils-the-bird-module-at-semicon-europa-2026/">micron-scale material deposition and defect repair</a> using maskless capillary printing and local laser sintering.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>AI and compute</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Expedera</strong> and <strong>Cadence</strong> teamed up to demonstrate an <a href="https://www.expedera.com/blog/2026/11/09/expedera-collaborates-with-cadence-to-expand-edge-ai-capabilities/">edge AI subsystem</a> in pre-silicon simulation, pairing Expedera&#8217;s NPU and Cadence&#8217;s AI co-processor and distributing workloads for execution across both IP blocks.</li>
<li><strong>MemryX AI</strong> released a new <a href="https://memryx.ai/news/memryx-ai-unveils-mx3-scaling-ai-inference-beyond-1-pops/">AI accelerator</a> with a distributed dataflow architecture designed to support larger models across edge devices, industrial systems, and high-density inference infrastructure.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Automotive and power electronics</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Keysight</strong> expanded its <a href="https://www.keysight.com/us/en/about/newsroom/catalog/news-release.2026.1110_pr26-125-keysight-expands-high-power-test-portfolio-for-broader-power-validation.html">high-power ATE</a> portfolio to support applications operating at up to 1,500V, including power-stage characterization, battery testing, charging systems, power conversion, and aerospace equipment.</li>
<li><strong>Infineon, Visteon, and Shinry</strong> are collaborating to develop an <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202611-030">efficient single-stage onboard charger</a> for EVs built with GaN BDS technology.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2 id="Research"><strong>Research</strong></h2>
<h3 data-pm-slice="1 1 []"><strong>2D transistors</strong></h3>
<ul data-spread="false">
<li><strong>NUS</strong>-led researchers developed <a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-11272-2">wafer-scale, single-crystal gate dielectrics for 2D transistors</a>, integrating titanium dioxide (TiO₂) with MoS₂ and WSe₂ channels. The devices achieved near-ideal switching, minimal hysteresis, and inverter static power consumption below 1 picowatt, potentially enabling ultra-low-power logic chips.</li>
<li><strong>Tsinghua University</strong> researchers developed a <a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-79139-8">room-temperature process for depositing gate dielectrics for 2D transistors</a> using a clay-based material. The process avoids high-temperature annealing after dielectric deposition and enables MoS₂ transistors operating at 1 volt with hysteresis as low as 1 millivolt. The team also demonstrated logic gates and SRAM, suggesting a potential route to lower-temperature chip fabrication.</li>
</ul>
<p>Researchers at <strong>UW–Madison</strong>, <strong>Texas A&amp;M</strong>, and the <strong>U.S. Army Research Lab</strong> developed <a href="https://engineering.wisc.edu/news/a-quantum-material-and-a-little-gold-leads-to-a-record-setting-digital-memory-device/">nonvolatile memory devices based on sliding ferroelectric tunnel junctions</a>. Using atomically thin molybdenum disulfide and engineered gold contacts, the devices achieved a reported 100,000-fold improvement in memory readout contrast, potentially enabling more energy-efficient memory.</p>
<p><strong>University of Rochester</strong> and <strong>Technion</strong> researchers developed a <a href="https://www.rochester.edu/newscenter/what-is-molybdenum-chemical-element-mo-metal-restructure-heat-732432/">method for altering molybdenum&#8217;s crystal structure at room temperature</a> using precisely controlled mechanical pressure. The technique creates new crystal grains without heating the metal to approximately 1,000°C, potentially enabling localized defect repair and improved materials for future semiconductor interconnects.</p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Security"><strong>Security</strong></h2>
<h3><strong>Alerts and vulnerabilities</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li>AI, chip and tech companies are recruiting ex-intelligence operatives to <a href="https://www.reuters.com/legal/litigation/help-wanted-ai-firms-court-ex-spies-nvidia-drama-spotlights-security-risks-2026-11-12/">combat espionage risks</a> in the wake of former <strong>Nvidia</strong> CEO Jensen Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy, Reuters reported.</li>
<li><strong>AMD</strong> disclosed three <a href="https://docs.cloud.google.com/support/bulletins">side-channel vulnerabilities in 5th-generation EPYC processors</a> that could expose sensitive data. Google Cloud has implemented protections between virtual machines but says customers need OS updates to address attacks within them.</li>
<li>AMD also issued <a href="https://www.amd.com/en/resources/product-security.html">security bulletins</a> covering firmware, UEFI/SMM, IOMMU, and transient-execution vulnerabilities, including a <a href="https://www.amd.com/en/resources/product-security/bulletin/amd-sb-3064.html?">RowHammer attack</a> targeting SEV-SNP memory protections.</li>
<li><strong>Intel</strong> disclosed <a href="https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/security-center/advisory/intel-sa-01554.html">multiple UEFI firmware vulnerabilities</a> affecting its D50DNP and M50FCP server boards, along with <a href="https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/security-center/advisory/intel-sa-01580.html">TDX Migration Trust Domain flaws</a> that include a critical authentication vulnerability. Intel released updated firmware and MigTD 0.7.2 to address the issues.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>New offerings</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Google DeepMind</strong> developed an agentic formal-verification framework that <a href="https://deepmind.google/blog/introducing-alphaproof-code/">proved key security properties of Signal’s cryptographic implementation</a> down to the compiled machine code running on devices. DeepMind says the approach eventually could be applied to systems such as medical-device firmware and power-grid software.</li>
<li><strong>AWS</strong> launched Nitro Sandboxes, adding <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/containers/aws-nitro-sandboxes-hardware-enforced-workload-isolation-on-amazon-eks/">hardware-isolated environments and cryptographic attestation</a> for AI workloads.</li>
<li><a href="https://www.ibm.com/new/announcements/ibm-plans-to-bring-anthropic-s-claude-mythos-cyber-reasoning-into-ibm-concert">IBM</a> and <a href="https://blogs.cisco.com/ai/cisco-ai-defense-to-add-anthropics-claude-mythos-for-ai-red-teaming">Cisco</a> announced plans to embed <strong>Anthropic</strong>’s Claude Mythos cybersecurity model into their security products.</li>
<li><strong>Lattice </strong>and<strong> NXP r</strong>eleased a <a href="https://www.latticesemi.com/About/Newsroom/PressReleases/2026/Lattice-and-NXP-Deliver-Flexible-Secure-Computing-Solution-for-the-Industrial-Edge">joint secure computing solution</a> for industrial edge AI applications.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Government security news</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>ENISA</strong> released a new report on the <a href="https://www.enisa.europa.eu/news/enisa-reveals-the-state-of-cybersecurity-in-the-union">state of cybersecurity</a> in the EU, evaluating regulatory compliance, cybersecurity system maturity, and future resilience across each member state.</li>
<li>French security officials <a href="https://www.diplomatie.gouv.fr/fr/presse-et-ressources/decouvrir-et-informer/actualites/declaration-d-attribution-a-l-iran-de-cyberattaques-visant-la-france?utm_source=newsletter&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_campaign=newsletter_axioscodebook&amp;stream=top&amp;lctg=6a459ea9c9d8f13094005247">blame Iran for cyberphysical attacks</a> that shut down refrigeration systems at more than 700 retail sites nationwide last month, but didn’t assign blame for the second wave of attacks that hit refrigeration systems across 11 European countries on Nov. 2.</li>
<li>President Trump nominated <strong>Palantir</strong> security chief Michael LoSapio to serve as <a href="https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2026/11/nominations-and-withdrawals-sent-to-the-senate-ba2b/?_sp=22010578-ba7a-40f2-8b4e-32945c8cc98e.1794501774329">the next CISA director</a>, pending Senate confirmation.</li>
<li><strong>CISA</strong>’s alerts for the week are <a href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories">here</a>.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Security technical papers</strong></h3>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []"><strong>Texas A&amp;M</strong> researchers proposed a 5G-based location-verification system for AI accelerators to help <a href="https://semiengineering.com/hardware-based-location-checks-target-ai-chip-smuggling-texas-am/">combat chip smuggling and enforce semiconductor export controls</a>. Called 5G CATS, the architecture would combine a 5G radio, cryptographic engine, and hardware interlock within the GPU package to verify chip locations and restrict unauthorized operation. Simulations showed improved detection of attempts to disguise a chip&#8217;s location using long-distance fiber relays.</p>
<p>And:</p>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08380">Sensor-to-Register Data Protection</a> for Resource-Constrained RISC-V Devices (<strong>Durham</strong>)</li>
<li>Protection Through Bounded <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12622">Delegation in RISC-V MCUs</a> (<strong>Minho, Infineon</strong>)</li>
<li>Private Boosted Decision Trees Ready for <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14317">Trusted HW &amp; Leakage-free Hyperparameters</a> (<strong>Luebeck, ETH Zurich</strong>)</li>
</ul>

<h2 id="Trending Video"><strong>Trending Video</strong></h2>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/memory-in-physical-ai/">Memory in Physical AI</a>: LPDDR6 becomes an essential ingredient as intelligence moves into autonomous and mobile edge systems.</p>
<p><strong>Cadence</strong>&#8216;s Frank Ferro explains why data hierarchies are so critical for physical AI, what can be done on the memory side to speed up read/write times, and how to maximize performance while keeping costs down.</p>
<p><iframe loading="lazy" src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/68oMarzN2xI" width="480" height="270" frameborder="0" allowfullscreen="allowfullscreen"></iframe></p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Quantum Hardware"><strong>Quantum Hardware</strong></h2>
<p><strong>IBM</strong> announced its upcoming <a href="https://newsroom.ibm.com/2026-11-11-ibm-quantum-nighthawk-demonstrates-new-levels-of-speed-and-quality,-with-plans-to-scale-beyond-1,000-qubits-in-2027">quantum processor</a>, which will be built to execute 10,000 quantum operations across 520 programmable qubits, with plans to link two into a single system.</p>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []"><strong>Rigetti Computing</strong> reported <a href="https://investors.rigetti.com/news-releases/news-release-details/rigetti-computing-reports-third-quarter-2026-financial-results">99.5% median two-qubit gate fidelity</a> on its 108-qubit superconducting processor, built from 12 interconnected nine-qubit chiplets. Internal testing also showed 99.7% median fidelity across a 50-qubit subset, demonstrating progress in modular QPU performance.</p>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []"><strong>Diraq</strong> deployed what it calls the <a href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/11/09/3398883/0/en/diraq-deploys-world-s-first-silicon-spin-quantum-computer-inside-a-commercial-data-center.html">first silicon-spin quantum computer in a commercial data center</a>, installing an eight-qubit system at Equinix in Sydney, Australia. Built using conventional semiconductor manufacturing processes, the system draws less than 20 kW, with plans to scale to 256 qubits within a year.</p>
<p><strong>Xanadu</strong> and <strong>HyperLight</strong> built a TFLN <a href="https://xanadu.ai/press/xanadu-and-hyperlight-achieve-breakthrough-reduction-in-optical-switch-loss">optical switch</a> with an insertion loss of 7 mdB, a nearly 3× improvement over the companies&#8217; previous effort to reduce qubit signal degradation in quantum photonics.</p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Workforce, Education"><strong>Workforce, Education</strong></h2>
<p>The <strong>SIA</strong> urged the Department of Homeland Security to <a href="https://www.semiconductors.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/SIA_OPT_Fee_Letter_Final.pdf?">withdraw proposed fees for Optional Practical Training (OPT)</a>, warning they could hurt U.S. semiconductor recruitment and manufacturing. The proposal would impose a $70K initial fee and $30K for subsequent OPT periods, including STEM extensions. SIA asked for exemptions for semiconductor and other critical-technology workers if the rule moves forward.</p>
<p><strong>SEMI Foundation</strong> launched its global <a href="https://www.semi.org/en/semi-foundation-launches-global-chip-the-future-challenge-to-inspire-the-next-generation-of-semiconductor-nnovators">Chip the Future Challenge</a>, inviting students to submit semiconductor research or develop solutions in chip design, manufacturing, AI hardware, advanced packaging and emerging technologies.</p>
<p><strong>Lam Research Foundation</strong> partnered with <strong>LEGO Education</strong> on a hands-on STEM initiative designed to <a href="https://newsroom.lamresearch.com/curiosity-is-building-lam-foundation-lego-education?blog=true">spark student interest in engineering and technology</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Qualcomm</strong> reportedly began <a href="https://www.tribuneindia.com/news/business/semiconductor-firm-qualcomm-begins-layoffs-in-india-around-1000-jobs-may-be-at-risk/">layoffs in India</a>, with around 1,000 jobs potentially affected.</p>

		<title>The Semiconductor Singularity</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/the-semiconductor-singularity/</link>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Accelerating IC innovation with AI.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-semiconductor-singularity/">The Semiconductor Singularity</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Semiconductor innovation is entering a new era defined by exponential feedback loops between hardware and software, a transformation Ray Kurzweil characterizes as the “semiconductor singularity.” While AI is rapidly dismantling long-standing bottlenecks in IC design and verification, true acceleration requires secure, explainable and trustworthy solutions from design through manufacturing. This paper explains how agentic AI systems, such as Siemens Calibre AI, enable rapid, auditable workflows, foster cross-team and cross-company collaboration and embed transparency and trust into every feedback loop. As the industry approaches the singularity, embedding trust becomes as vital as technical advancement, ensuring sustainable, scalable semiconductor progress.</p>
<h2>What you&#8217;ll learn:</h2>
<ul>
<li>How agentic AI removes verification bottlenecks in semiconductor workflows</li>
<li>Why trust and transparency are crucial for sustainable innovation</li>
<li>The future of collaborative IC signoff enabled by secure AI platforms</li>
</ul>
<h2>Who should read this:</h2>
<ul>
<li>Semiconductor executives and digital transformation leaders</li>
<li>IC design and verification managers seeking workflow acceleration</li>
<li>EDA technology strategists and engineers</li>
<li>Business decision-makers focused on secure innovation</li>
</ul>
<p>To read more, click <a href="https://resources.sw.siemens.com/en-US/white-paper-the-semiconductor-singularity-accelerating-ic-innovation-with-ai/">here</a>.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-semiconductor-singularity/">The Semiconductor Singularity</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
